In practice, an interaction is sought using estimates provided by a therapeutic trial. The most
suitable  study  design,  although  rarely  encountered  in  practice,  is  presented  in  section  3.1.
The statistical uncertainty of estimates of the treatment effect obtained for each modality of
the  factor  studied  (for  example  a  marker)  must  then  be  considered.  Indeed,  the  values
observed for the treatment effect can be different, without this being a reality; it may be just
an  artefact  due  to  random  errors.  Therefore,  in  practice,  to  be  able  to  prove  that  the  two
estimates of a treatment effect obtained as a function of the factor studied (when it is binary)
are  actually  different  from  one  another,  it  is  suitable  to  test  the  statistical  reality  of  the
interaction  by  an  ad-hoc  statistical  test.  This  is  especially  what  is  commonly  done  in
subgroup  analyses.  In  fact,  it  is  only  possible  to  talk  about  interaction  if  it  is  statistically
significant, that is, the difference between the two treatment values obtained is statistically
significant.  In  this  test,  the  null  hypothesis  is  that  the  treatment  has  the  same  effect
regardless of the value of the third factor. The p-value calculated is the probability of wrongly
rejecting  the  null  hypothesis.  This  test  seeks  to  discover  whether  or  not  the  differences
observed between the values can be reasonably attributed to chance (see Figure 3).

Interactions can be sought for binary, qualitative or continuous factors. Most often they are
binary  factors2  because  this  is  the  simplest  and  most  practical  case.  When  the  marker  is
continuous, the search for interactions uses regression techniques (logistic regression or Cox

2 At this time, in the field of alleged "companion" tests, the markers are very substantially binary. Therefore, we
limited  our  scope  to  this  type  of  marker  in  the  context  of  the  methodological  guides  relating  to  these  test
assessments (the present guide as well as the guide entitled "Companion test associated with a targeted therapy:
definitions and method").

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
15

model,  for  example).  It  can  also  be  done  by  transforming  the  continuous  variable  into  an
ordinal variable.

Given  the  precision  of  the  estimates  of
these  two  risk  ratios,  their  difference  is

 The  concept  of  qualitative  interaction  has  been  derived  from  the  concept  of  quantitative
interaction.  There  is  a  qualitative  interaction  when  a  quantitative  interaction  leads  to  a
change  of  direction  in  the  treatment  effect  according  to  the  factor  studied.  This  situation  is
observed when the treatment is beneficial for one modality of the factor and detrimental for
the other (see Table 5). The strength of this qualitative interaction is graded by the statistical
significance  of  the  treatment  effects.  The  strongest  qualitative  interaction  is  produced  by  a
situation  where  the  treatment  is  statistically  significantly  beneficial  for  one  modality  of  the
factor and statistically significantly detrimental for the other.

1.4  Companion test concept

A  companion  test  is  a  diagnostic  test  permitting  selection  only  of  patients  in  whom  a
treatment  is  likely  to  provide  a  benefit  among  those  diagnosed  with  a  given  illness,
according to their status for a predictive marker identified by this test. For this reason, the
test is considered a "companion" to the use of the treatment.

As  discussed  in  the  foreword  to  this  document,  the  assessment  of  this  approach  can  only
logically rely on a joint  assessment of the test and the treatment with two objectives: to
show that the treatment is only effective in marker (+) patients and that the companion test
improves therapeutic decision-making and therefore patient clinical outcome.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
16

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

It is important to emphasise that the benefit of selecting patients presumed to be the better,
or  even  the  only,  responders  to  a  treatment  by  a  companion  test  is  also  to  optimise  the
clinicalefficiency3  of  this  treatment  compared  with  a  strategy  consisting  of  using  the
treatment  in  all  patients.  Indeed,  the  selection  of  patients  on  the  basis  of  identification  of  a
marker that is not very or not at all predictive by a diagnostic test, which in this case cannot
be called a companion test, does not optimise clinical efficiency much, if at all. It may cause
a loss of opportunity for some patients if the treatment is actually more or less beneficial in
everyone. The rest of this document will show that this ethical problem is of major concern in
the context of assessing the utility of these tests.

1.5  Concepts of "targeted" and "stratified" therapies

The  therapeutic  approach  related  to  the  use  of  predictive  markers  is  sometimes  called
personalised  medicine  or  individualized  medicine.  These  names  should  be  considered
misnomers  because  the  approach  involving  predictive  markers  remains  populational  and
statistical, rather than individual, and is actually a stratified medicine or precision medicine
approach  (4).  It  merely  refines  the  estimates  by  focusing  on  subpopulations  of  patients
(hence the term stratification) and thus produces an average estimate of the treatment effect
in these subpopulations (strata), not an estimate of the treatment effect in an individual.

Targeted  therapy  is  a  treatment  providing  benefits  only  in  certain  patients  identified  by  a
predictive marker detected by a companion test.

In  practice,  it  appears  that  claims  that  a  treatment  is  targeted  may  be  observed  in  two
situations:

  when  the  targeting  was  sought  by  the  developer  at  the  time  of  treatment  design



(synthesis): this is called targeting "by construction";
following  exploratory  research  by  a  retrospective  analysis  of  the  results  from  one  (or
more) trial(s) conducted on a population not selected by a marker, followed  or not by a
prospective confirmatory trial (see section 3.2.3).

The  targeting  "by  construction"  approach  has  historically  been  the  first  used.  It  still  is
today.  Most  often,  the  efficacy  of  compounds  claimed  to  fall  within  this  concept  is  only
studied  in  patients  carrying  a  marker  designated  as  a  target.  The  lack  of  efficacy  of  the
compound in marker (-) patients is considered implicit. However, the predictive value of the
marker  cannot  reasonably  rely  on  implicit  foundations,  strictly  speaking, but  only  on  clinical
evidence.  Indeed,  preclinical  data  represent  only  preliminary  exploratory  data  that  very
roughly reflect the data that will be obtained in humans. In fact, due to the great complexity
and peculiarities of the human organism, no type of preclinical study (immortalized cell lines,
animal studies, etc.) can precisely and reliably predict the clinical effects of a drug. Moreover,
to consider implicit the lack of efficacy of a treatment in marker (-) patients would mean being
able to demonstrate an indisputable specificity of the treatment towards its target in vivo. Yet,
to provide such a demonstration appears very difficult.

As it happens, we now have some experience with the first therapies qualified as targeted. It
has revealed several examples where, although the treatment had been initially considered
to  be  a  priori  specific  for  its  target  and  the  marker  to  be  central  in  the  treatment
mechanism of action, the treatment appeared active in a substantial proportion of marker (-)
patients for  a variety  of possible  reasons. This  is  in particular  the case with  trastuzumab  in
breast  cancer,  for  which  efficacy  in  HER2/neu  (human  epidermal  growth  factor  receptor  2)
negative cancers is envisaged (5–7). A second example is cetuximab. This other monoclonal
antibody  was  synthesised  to  target  the  EGFR  (epidermal  growth  factor  receptor),  an

3 Clinical efficiency corresponds to the number of events avoided for a certain number of treated patients. It does
not involve any economic dimension. It is an absolute benefit generated by the treatment of a population with a
given intervention. It may be viewed as a measure of purely clinical efficacy.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
17

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

oncogenic  tyrosine  kinase  receptor  (TKR)  detected  in  70-85%  of  colorectal  cancers  by  the
immunohistochemical  (IHC)  method.  Yet,  currently,  the  analysis  of  data  from  certain  trials
has shown that up to 25% of patients with metastatic colorectal cancer identified as "EGFR
negative"  by  IHC  could  respond  to  cetuximab  (8).  Two  main  explanations  were  suggested:
the poor specificity of the IHC technique and/or an ADCC (antibody dependent cell-mediated
cytotoxicity)  phenomenon  linked  to  a  toxic  effect  of  cetuximab  toward  tumour  cells.  Aside
from monoclonal antibodies, it is fairly common not to observe a real specificity for a unique
target  within  the  other  large  class  of  the  so-called  “targeted”  therapies  in  oncology,
represented  by  the  tyrosine  kinase  inhibitors  (TKI).  For  example,  crizotinib  is  used  as  an
inhibitor  for  a  TKR  called  ALK  (anaplastic  lymphoma  kinase)  and  its  oncogenic  variants  in
non small  cell  lung  cancer.  However,  it  also  inhibits  the  receptor for  the hepatocyte growth
factor  (HGFR/c-Met)  also  involved  in  oncogenic  processes.  In  its  assessment  report
(EMA;
associated  with  crizotinib
EMA/CHMP/497137/2012)  clearly  stated  the  only  relative  specificity  of  this  drug:  "When
tested to its selectivity for different kinases, crizotinib was relatively specific to c-Met/HGFR
and ALK fusion proteins". These examples clearly show that "targeting by construction" is not
sufficient to justify the absence of clinical study in marker (-) patients. The lack of treatment
efficacy  in  these  patients  must  be  clinically  proven  to  attest  in  vivo  the  reality  of  the
targeting.  The  risk  of  wrongly  accepting  that  a  new  treatment  is  targeted  would  indeed
directly lead to a loss of opportunity for these patients.

the  European  Medicine  Agency

registration,

The  approach  of  claiming  that  a  treatment  is  targeted  following  a  post-hoc  exploratory
search  for  predictive  markers  is  detailed  in  section  3.2.  It  is  interesting  to  note  that,  in  a
certain way, this avenue of research has broadened the concept of "targeted” feature of
a drug. Indeed, this concept originally referred, in connection with "targeting by construction",
to the synthesis of treatments oriented toward molecular targets involved in their mechanism
of action and implying a direct interaction between the drug and its target. However, post-hoc
searches for predictive markers have led to situations where the initial “physical” target of the
drug, supposed to be targeted by construction, and the presumed predictive marker differ. It
led  to  a  broadening  from  the  concept  of  “physical”  pharmacological  target  to  the  more
general concept of “predictive marker”. This extension of the concept poses actually no real
concern as regards consistency since molecular targets, in theory, are also supposed to be
predictive  markers  (although  this  is  not  always  the  case,  see  section  3.2.3  "Search  for  a
predictive  marker  after  an  inconclusive  trial").  However,  it  still  must  be  noted  that  with  the
broadening  of  the  concept  to  predictive  markers  in  general,  the  understanding  of  the
mechanisms of the involvement of a marker in the efficacy of a given treatment, as well as
the overall mechanism of action of the treatment, has become more complex.

1.6  The various situations associated in practice with the

restriction of indication of a treatment to a subpopulation of
patients

"Targeting"  is  not  really  a  novel  approach.  This  concept  has  evolved  following  a  common
thread through therapeutic research and, in practice, restricting the administration of  a new
treatment  to  a  subpopulation  of  patients  is  not  limited  to  the  field  of  targeted  therapies.
Initially,  it  was  primarily  done  on  the  basis  of  clinical  criteria  and/or  biological  markers  and
obvious  mechanisms,  without  always  making  use  of  a  predictive  marker  (9).  What  has
changed dramatically appears to be an intensification of research in this field of therapeutics,
with a much finer level of mechanisms, entailing a risk of an explosion of wrong targeting.

Five main situations may be identified. This document only concerns cases 3, 4 and 5.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
18

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

1.  Restriction  of  the  administration  of  a  treatment  to  a  subgroup  at  risk  in  order  to
optimise clinical efficiency4 of this treatment in terms of number of events avoided per treated
patient (measured in absolute risk reduction (ARR) or number needed to treat (NNT)). This is
the  case,  for  example,  when  restricting  the  use  of  statins  in  primary  prevention  to  subjects
who  have  at  least  one  additional  risk  factor  relative  to  their  elevated  LDL  cholesterol.  This
restriction  to  a  particular  subgroup  may  be  decided  a  posteriori  by  a  reasoning  seeking  to
maximise the clinical efficiency of the treatment in terms of ARR or NNT. It is “mathematical”
and  therefore  does  not  need  particular  demonstration  by  the  facts.  This  aspect  is  not
discussed in this document. This approach is used, for example, by Dorresteijn et al. in  the
context of statins and stable coronary diseases (10).

2.  Selection  of  a  group  of  patients  posing  a  particular  therapeutic  problem  for  the
administration  of  a  treatment  (refining  of  the  nosological  entity  considered).  For
example,  in  the  prevention  of  stroke  recurrence,  it  was  apparent  very  early  on  that  the
existence  of  carotid  stenosis  could  be  a  promising  therapeutic  target  to  stratify  post-stroke
into  different  entities  depending  on  the  existence  of  a  tight  stenosis  and  the  possibility  of
performing  endarteriectomy  surgery.  Moreover,  this  permits  refining  the  use  of  antiplatelet
agents  by  making  the  distinction  between  carotid  stenosis  and  atrial  fibrillation.  This
approach has been used without formalisation in numerous fields. This is even more natural
for  a  clinician  than  a more general  approach  mixing  different  types  of  patients  in  the  same
trial.

3.  Targeting  an  indispensable  and  non-ubiquitous  element  involved  in  the  treatment
mechanism  of  action,  for  example  a molecular  target  that  does  not  exist  in  all  patients  in
accordance  with  the  existence  of  several  genetic  variants.  It  corresponds  to  the  targeting
called  "by  construction",  the  target  having  been  the  keystone  to  the  therapeutic  idea.  The
underlying idea is that there is no reason to treat patients not carrying this target because no
benefit  must  be  a  priori  expected  in  them  due  to  the  mechanism  of  action.  This  debatable
point is discussed in section 1.5.

4.  Targeting according to a presumed predictive marker empirically discovered (not a
priori  envisaged  but  a  posteriori  sought,  in  particular  following  inconclusive  results  in  the
entire  population).  Due  to  the  post-hoc  and  non  hypothetico-deductive  nature  of  the
identification of this marker, a confirmation test is absolutely required to assert its predictive
value.

5.  Proposition of a posteriori targeting on a presumed predictive marker for a treatment
having already proven its benefit for purposes of optimising the use of this treatment. One
example  of  this  is  measuring  residual  platelet  activity  with  the  VerifyNow®  device  for
optimising clopidogrel treatment (11,12).

1.7  The potential benefits of targeting a therapy

The  benefit  of  targeting  a  therapy  using  a  predictive  marker  is  particularly  to  optimise  the
clinical efficiency of the treatment relative to a strategy of using the treatment in all patients.
Without  this  result  in  terms  of  optimising  clinical  efficiency,  pseudo-targeting  may  lead  to
additional  expenses,  costing  the  healthcare  system  time  and  energy  with  no  public  health
return,  and  causing  a  loss  of  opportunity  for  some  patients  if  the  treatment  is  actually
beneficial in all patients.

Four targeting scenarios are detailed in this section with their populational consequences:

4 See note 3.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
19

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix



right targeting: the marker used to select patients to treat is a true predictive marker with
real clinical utility;

  wrong targeting using a marker that is not even a treatment effect moderator;
  pseudo-targeting using a marker that is a treatment effect moderator but which does not
completely differentiate patients who benefit from the treatment from those who do not;
targeting using an interaction on safety.



A summary of results is presented in section 1.7.5.

1.7.1  Right targeting

Table 8 illustrates the populational consequences of appropriate targeting of a therapy on a
true predictive marker (the treatment is effective in marker (+) patients and has no efficacy in
marker (-) patients.

This simulation is performed with the following parameters. The marker is present in 60% of
the subjects of the population (line 1 of the table). The risk without treatment of the endpoint
event is 10% (line 2) in marker (+) and marker (-) patients. This marker is therefore not a risk
factor. The treatment is only effective in marker (+) patients where it leads to a risk reduction
of 20% (RR=0.80, line 3). It has no effect in marker (-) patients (line 4).

Without treatment, a population of 1000 patients (line 5)  will have 100  events (line 8)  while
with  treatment  of  all  patients  or  only  marker  (+)  patients  (targeted  strategy)  only  88  events
will occur.

Therefore,  the  benefit  of  the  targeted  strategy  appears  in  terms  of  number  of  events
avoided  per  treated  patient  (line  17).  Relative  to  no  treatment,  the  targeted  strategy
prevents 12 events (line 15) with the treatment of only 600 subjects (60% of the population)
versus 12 events avoided with the treatment of all 1000 patients. Thus, the number of events
avoided per patient treated is 0.02 (20 per 1000) with the targeted strategy versus 0.012 (12
per  1000)  with  the  undifferentiated  strategy.  The  targeted  strategy  prevents  67%  more
events for the same number of  treated subjects than the undifferentiated strategy (line 18).
Moreover,  given  that  the  treatment  has  no  benefit  in  marker  (-)  subjects,  the  targeted
strategy  avoids  all  avoidable  events  (line  16)  with  the  treatment  of  only  60%  of  the
population.

Another  benefit  of  targeting  may  be  to  optimise  the  overall  benefit/risk  balance  by  not
subjecting  patients  who  cannot  benefit  from  the  treatment  to  adverse  effects  (marker  (-)
patients in this case). For example, if the risk of a serious adverse effect (SAE) is 1.2% in all
patients  (Mk+  and  Mk-),  treating  the  total  population  would  lead,  on  average,  to  the
occurrence of 12 SAEs  (per 1000  treated patients). The benefit/risk balance would then be
12  events  avoided  for  12  induced  SAEs,  therefore  a  benefit/risk  balance  of  no  interest.  In
contrast,  a  targeted  treatment  would  only  cause  7.2  SAEs  on  average (1.2%  *  600  treated
patients) corresponding to a benefit/risk balance of 12 events avoided for 7 induced SAEs, or
0.6 SAE induced per avoided event. In order to better understand this issue, trials should use
an endpoint with a clear clinical benefit as primary efficacy endpoint.

1.7.2  Wrong targeting

Table  9  illustrates  the  use  of  a  strategy  wrongly  targeted  on  a  non-predictive  marker.  The
treatment here has the same efficacy in marker (+) and (-) patients (lines 3 and 4).

In this situation, the main disadvantage of the targeted  strategy is that it does not prevent
all avoidable events (compared with the treatment of all patients) but only 60% of them (line
16).

strategy.

1.7.3  Targeting with a partially predictive marker (pseudo-targeting)

This  simulation  (see  Table  10)  illustrates  the  populational  consequences  of  targeting  done
with  a  treatment  effect  moderator  that  is  not  a  true  predictive  marker,  i.e.  which  does  not
completely separate the patients in whom the treatment is effective from those in whom it is
not. The treatment is still effective, but less so, in marker (-) patients.

In this situation, the treatment leads to a relative reduction in the risk of the endpoint event of
20% in Mk+ subjects and 10% in Mk- subjects (lines 3 and 4). A treatment targeted on Mk+
subjects would avoid only 75% of all avoidable events (treatment of all patients) (line 16) but
the  clinical  efficiency  of  the  treatment  (in  terms  of  events  avoided  per  patient  treated)
increases from -0.016 (16 events avoided per 1000 treatments) to 0.02 (20 events avoided
per 1000 treatments). In contrast, the total number of events observed in the total population
will be larger (88) than if all the patients were treated (84 events).

1.7.4  Stratification according to a safety interaction

Table  11  illustrates  the  introduction  of  a  targeted  strategy  using  a  predictive  marker  that
would predict the adverse events (at least one) related to a treatment. In marker (+) patients,
the treatment is safe with a low frequency of adverse events of 0.5% (line 3), while in marker
(-) patients, this risk is 8 times higher (4%, line 4).

The treatment of all patients (undifferentiated strategy) leads to a less favourable benefit/risk
balance with 1.1 events avoided for 1 induced adverse effect: 20 events avoided (reduction
from 100 to 80) for 19 induced adverse events. In contrast, with a targeting strategy, this ratio
is 4 events avoided for 1 induced adverse effect: 12 events avoided (88 instead of 100 with
no  treatment)  for  only  3  adverse  events  induced.  However,  the  targeted  strategy  is  less
efficient in terms of efficacy by avoiding only 60% of all avoidable events. Targeting reduces
the  number  of  adverse  events  by  84%  but  prevents  only  60%  of  the  avoidable  events.  In
practice,  this  type  of  targeting  is  commonly  done,  for  example  by  adding  treatment
contraindications.

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

2.  General principles for validating a diagnostic test

determining patient status for a presumed
predictive marker

2.1  The three levels of validation for a diagnostic test

The  assessment  of  a  diagnostic  test  determining  the  status  of  patients  for  a  presumed
predictive marker consists of three levels: assessing the analytic validity, clinical validity and
clinical utility of the test (13–15).

  The analytic validity of a diagnostic test is its ability to perform in vitro the measurement
of interest with accuracy and reliability. In other words: does the test really measure what
it is supposed to measure, and does it do so correctly? This validation includes studying
analytical  sensitivity  and  specificity,  reproducibility,  robustness  and  satisfying  quality
controls.

  The clinical validity of a diagnostic test is its ability to accurately and reliably predict the
clinical phenotype of interest (for example: overall survival or progression-free survival of
patients receiving a given treatment): is there a relationship and what is it, between the
results  of  the  test  and  the  phenotype  of  interest?  Clinical  validity  includes  clinical
sensitivity  and  specificity,  as  well  as  the  positive  and  negative  predictive  values  of  the
test.  These  parameters  may  be  combined  under  the  name  "diagnostic  performance"  of
the test.

  The clinical utility of a diagnostic test is its ability to improve the patient clinical outcome
in  measurable  clinical  events,  and  to  provide  an  added  value  in  terms  of  optimising
treatment decisions and, as a corollary, therapeutic strategy.

The  study  of  analytic  validity  and  clinical  validity  are  general  concepts  for  diagnostic  tests,
whether or not a marker is involved. They are beyond the scope of this document, which is
dedicated to tests that identify predictive markers.

The  demonstration  of  clinical  utility  is  an  indispensable  element  in  the  field  of
companion  testing.  This  demonstration  is  needed  because  it  attests  to  the  added  value
provided  by  the  test  compared  with  a  diagnostic  test  that  only  permits  selection  of  a
subpopulation by identification of a marker. The very concept of companion test implies that
its clinical utility has been demonstrated in that it obviates the pointless treatment of patients
in  whom  the  treatment  will  be  ineffective,  and  conversely,  does  not  lead  to  a  loss  of
opportunity  by  identification  of  a  marker  that  is  not  predictive  or  not  sufficiently  predictive,
thereby excluding patients from a treatment that could actually benefit them.

2.2  The three conditions absolutely required to demonstrate the

clinical utility of a diagnostic test

Demonstrating the clinical utility of a diagnostic test involves demonstrating the following
three elements (9,16–19):






the marker identified by the test must change the effect of the treatment (existence of a
"marker/treatment" interaction);
the treatment must be effective in marker (+) patients;
the treatment must have no clinical benefit in marker (-) patients.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
26

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

2.2.1  Demonstrating marker by treatment interaction

In  statistical  terms,  a  predictive  marker  must  be  a  treatment  effect  moderator  (see  section
1.2.1) i.e. the extent of the treatment effect must vary according to whether or not the marker
is present (in the case of a binary marker). Therefore, if the treatment effect is measured by a
relative  risk  (or  hazard  ratio)  this  means  having,  in  the  subgroup  of  marker  (+)  and
marker  (-)  patients,  two  statistically  different  relative  risks.  This  statistically  significant
difference between the two subgroups statistically validates the treatment effect moderation
depending on whether or not the marker is present, in other words the "marker by treatment
interaction". This  search  for  an  interaction is  similar  to  that  done  in any  subgroup analysis,
whether or not the objective is to show a predictive marker.

This situation is illustrated in Table 12 with the example of a new treatment assessed versus
placebo in add-on to the usual strategy. The efficacy endpoint is total mortality.

Table  12  -  Illustration  of  a  statistical  interaction  suggesting  the  existence  of  a  predictive
marker.

Treatment N

Placebo

Risk Ratio
(95% CI)

Marker +

325/2000

445/2000

0.73 [0.64; 0.83]

Marker -

440/2000

465/2000

0.94 [0.84; 1.06]

Interaction p=0.0033

The  interaction  test  only  answers  the  question  of  whether  or  not  the  existence  of  an
interaction is demonstrated. It is also possible, to a certain extent, to give an estimate of the
extent of the interaction. One possibility is to use the relative risk ratio RαRR.

Using the data from Table 12, RRR=0.73/0.94=0.78. This means that the treatment effect in
marker (-) patients is only 0.78 times that in marker (+) patients, corresponding to a relative
reduction  in  efficacy  of  22  %.  In  practice,  therefore,  the  confidence  interval  for  this  ratio
should be calculated to reflect the precision with which this index is known. This makes use
of the same approach as the one for indirect comparisons. This confidence interval therefore
reflects the uncertainty existing in the estimate of both relative risks that constitute it.

The value of this relative risk ratio gives an idea of the clinical relevance of the interaction.
Indeed,  an  interaction  of  small  but  significant  amplitude  will  not  lead  to  a  substantial
optimisation of clinical efficiency with the strategy using a marker. Nevertheless, it should be
emphasised  that  the  link  between  the  quantitative  extent  of  the  interaction  and  the  clinical
efficiency of the targeted strategy is not very simple (see Table 9; Table 10; Table 11). In this
instance,  the  quantitative  extent  of  this  interaction  is  not  an  element  in  itself  of  the
demonstration of the predictive value of the marker.

2.2.2  Demonstrating the treatment efficacy in marker (+) patients

The trial should logically demonstrate the efficacy of the treatment in marker (+) patients, by
following  the  same  criteria  as  for  a  conventional  therapeutic  trial:  prospective  trial,  free
from bias, statistically significant, clinically relevant and having a good external consistency
(biological plausibility and consistency among available studies).

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
27

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

A specific aspect relative to a conventional trial concerning demonstrating the efficacy of a
new treatment is alpha risk control. Indeed, a latent multiplicity of statistical tests is inherent
in  this  trial  design:  testing  the  treatment  effect  in  marker  (+),  marker  (-)  and/or  all  patients.
Each time, a 5% risk of yielding false conclusive results exists in the absence of precaution.
Alpha risk inflation can be controlled in several ways. The first is a hierarchical sequential
analysis  where  marker  (+)  patients  are  tested  first,  then  all  patients.  The  second  is  to  pre-
specify explicitly that the co-primary objectives of the trial are to validate the interaction and
efficacy  of  treatment  in  marker  (+)  patients  by  concentrating  the  alpha  risk  on  the  analysis
relating  to  these  primary  objectives.  However,  the  analyses  in  all  patients  and  marker  (-)
patients will raise the problem of multiple statistical comparisons. Finally, a third possibility is
to  adjust  the  alpha  risk  taking  into  account  the  multiple  potential  comparisons  using  a
Bonferroni correction, or the like.

2.2.3  Demonstrating the lack of treatment efficacy in marker (-) patients

► Principle

The existence of an interaction between the effect of the treatment and the marker does not
mean that the treatment  has  no  effect  in marker  (-)  patients.  In fact,  when  an  interaction  is
detected, it is completely possible (see Table 13) that the treatment is effective both in
marker (+) and in marker (-) patients with only a difference in the extent of the effect.

Table 13 - Situation where an interaction exists and where the treatment is effective in marker
(+) and marker (-) patients.

The  concept  of  predictive  marker  implies  a  lack  of  effect  of  the  treatment  in  marker  (-)
patients. Obtaining a risk ratio (or relative risk, RR) close to 1 in the marker (-) subgroup , as
is the case in the example of Table 14, is a suggestive argument, but is not sufficient to rule
out the lack of treatment effect in these patients with reasonable certainty.

Table  14  -  Predictive  marker  associated  with  a  strong  suggestion  of  no  effect  in  marker  (-)
patients.

Treatment N

RR (95% CI)

Marker +

390/2000

A  statistical  demonstration  of  the  lack  of treatment  effect  in this  subgroup  is necessary,
based  on  the  study  of  the  confidence  interval  for  the  relative  risk  of  these  patients.  A
non-statistically significant result can simply arise from a lack of power. Moreover, subgroup
analyses generate beta risk inflation. This demonstration must make use of a non-inferiority
study based on the confidence interval of the relative risk of these patients, showing that it is
possible  to  rule  out  with  a  97.5%  degree  of  certainty  that  the  treatment  effect  could  be
greater  than  a  certain  value  corresponding  to  the  minimal  clinically  useful  value.  The
reasoning  is  based,  in  practice,  on  reasoning  mirroring  that  for  non-superiority  (with  a

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
28

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

clinical  non-superiority  limit).  Non-superiority  can  be  concluded  when  the  lower  limit  of  the
confidence interval for the treatment effect in the strata of marker (-) patients is greater than
the  non-superiority  limit.  In fact,  we  are  looking  to  rule  out  the  possibility  that  the  treatment
would be  effective (i.e.  that  it  would have an  effect  substantially  superior  to  the  placebo)  in
marker (-) patients.

Figure  4  shows  the  illustration  of  the  application  of  this  non-superiority  reasoning  to
demonstrate the predictive value of a marker. In the case of result C, it is impossible to rule
out the treatment having an effect in marker (-) subjects because the confidence interval is
very  broad  and  compatible  with  substantial  effects  (all  of  its  part  below  the  non-superiority
limit).  The  situation  sought  is  that  of  result  A,  where  it  is  possible  to  rule  out  that  the
treatment could be sufficiently effective to be clinically beneficial in these patients. Result B
does  not  permit  drawing  conclusions  since  it  remains  compatible  with  a  clinically  relevant
efficacy in these patients.

Limit of
non-superiority

Figure  4  -  Application  of  non-superiority  reasoning  to  demonstrate  the  predictive  value  of  a
marker. Examples of different situations for the marker (-) subgroup of patients.

It should be noted that this reasoning has not been regularly followed during the registration
of  the  first  so-called  “targeted”  treatments.  For  example,  cetuximab  was  registered  as  a
metastatic colorectal cancer treatment limited to patients with no KRAS gene mutation from a
simple estimate of the hazard ratio  close to 1 for progression-free survival in the marker (-)
subgroup (see section 0).

► Discussion regarding this approach

It  must  be  highlighted  that,  strictly  speaking,  the  demonstration  of  the  treatment  benefit  in
marker (+) patients would require the confidence interval to be entirely below the limit of non-
superiority. Indeed, the result obtained in marker (+) patients could otherwise be compatible
with the extent of the benefit by definition considered clinically insufficient (the part of the CI
above  the  non-superiority  limit).  This  amounts  to  obtaining  a  strong  interaction  that
completely separates the two confidence intervals obtained from the marker (+) and marker
(-) patients.

Moreover, this demonstration of non-superiority has, in practice, two critical points requiring
special attention.

The first point concerns the setting of the non-superiority limit, i.e. the smallest clinically
relevant difference. This limit is set arbitrarily and must be done a priori, before knowledge of

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
29

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

the  study  results  is  obtained.  Furthermore,  it  must  be  clinically  justified.  Setting  this  limit  is
greatly  facilitated  by  reasoning  in  terms  of  benefit/risk  balance  rather  than  in  terms  of
efficacy. In fact, it is very probable that there will be the same safety profile regardless of the
marker  status,5  therefore  the  benefit/risk  balance  will  be  less  favourable  in  marker  (-)
patients.  Thus,  concluding  inefficacy  in  marker  (-)  patients  could  be  easier  to  do  using  an
endpoint of net benefit aggregating both efficacy and risk (for example, an endpoint grouping
cardiovascular  events  and  major  bleeding  for  an  antiplatelet  agent  in  preventing  events  in
patients  suffering  from coronary disease).  This  sensitisation  of  the  measuring  tool  will  shift
the confidence interval to the right. The simplest case would be the one where a statistically
significant  inferiority  would  be  demonstrated  (range  completely  above  1).  If  this  net  benefit
endpoint  is  used at this level,  the  same endpoint  must  be  used to seek  the  interaction  and
demonstrate the benefit of the treatment in marker (+) patients.

The  second  point  is  related  to  the  objective  of  this  search.  Indeed,  the  objective  of
demonstrating that a new treatment would not be effective in marker (-) patients may raise an
ethical question. This point is discussed in section 3.1 of this guide, as part of the "marker
by treatment" study design.

2.3  Efficacy endpoints

The efficacy endpoints for studies conducted to validate the clinical utility of diagnostic tests
are the same endpoints used to assess treatment efficacy, i.e. clinical criteria.

Some  studies  seek  predictive  markers  only  on  intermediate  endpoints,  such  as  LDL
cholesterol  for  a  cholesterol-lowering  treatment  aimed  at  preventing  cardiovascular  events,
or  tumour  response  for  a  cancer  treatment.  This  approach  is  not  satisfactory  because  it
assumes  that  what  happens  at  the  intermediate  endpoint  is  directly  predictive  of  what
happens  at  the  clinical  endpoint  (both  in  positive  and  negative  ways).  A  positive  treatment
effect on the intermediate endpoint would thus be predictive of its clinical benefit and a lack
of effect on this intermediate endpoint would be predictive of a lack of clinical benefit. This is
a  very  strong  assumption,  not  acceptable  for  the  demonstration  of  treatment  efficacy.
Therefore,  there  is  no  reason  that  it  should  be  more  acceptable  for  seeking  a  predictive
marker.

2.4  Consequences of poor diagnostic performance on

demonstrating the test clinical utility

Figure 5 illustrates graphically the consequences on the estimates of the treatment efficacy
as a function of the diagnostic performance of the test used to determine patient status with
regard  to  the  marker.  These  graphs  represent  the  changes  in  treatment  effect  (risk  ratio)
observed  in  the  test  (+)  stratum  (patients  identified  as  marker  (+)  by  the  test)  and  test  (-)
stratum (patients identified as marker (-)) depending on the false positive rate (FPR) and the
false negative rate (FNR) given by the test. The real risk ratio in true marker (+) subjects is
0.8 and that in true marker (-) subjects is 1.00.

5 This is usually the case with predictive markers for efficacy.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
30

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

FNR

FPR

FNR

FPR

Figure  5  -  Changes  in  estimates  of  treatment  effect  (risk  ratio,  RR)  in  the  test  (+)  and  test  (-)
strata  depending  on  the  false  positive  rate  (FPR)  and  false  negative  rate  (FNR).  The  real  risk
ratio in marker (+) patients is 0.8 and that in marker (-) patients is 1.00.

It appears that a lack of sensitivity (increase in FNR) does not interfere with the estimate of
the  treatment  effect  in  the  (+)  stratum.  However,  it  leads  to  an  overestimate  in  the  test  (-)
stratum  that  could  lead  to  attenuation  of  the  interaction  between  the  two  strata  and  to
wrongly not concluding the predictive value of the marker (measured by this test).

A  lack  of  specificity  (increase  in  FPR)  does  not  lead  to  a  measurement  error  for  the
treatment effect in the test (-) stratum. Yet, it leads to an underestimate of the effect in the
test (+) stratum that can result in wrongly concluding a lack of treatment effect in the test (+)
stratum and therefore to an overall lack of treatment effect (risk ratio near 1 in both cases).

Note  that  the  lack  of  specificity  is  conservative  and does  not  lead to wrongly  accepting the
predictive  value  of  a  marker  and/or  the  efficacy  of  a  treatment.  In  contrast,  the  lack  of
sensitivity is conservative with regard to the predictive value but in theory can lead to wrongly
concluding  the  efficacy  of  the  treatment  in  all  patients  (test  (+)  and  test  (-))  while  it  would
really only be effective in the test (+) stratum.

In practice,  the  problems  related  to the  lack  of diagnostic  performance of  the  tests  used to
determine  the  status  of  patients  relative  to  a  marker  constitute  more  an  issue  for  the
developer than for regulatory authorities. In fact, lack of sensitivity and specificity contradict
the  hypothesis  of  predictive  value  of  the  marker  tested  in  the  interaction  trial.  The  only
situation directly involving authorities is that of the lack of sensitivity leading to conclude the
benefit of the treatment in all patients, including those of the test (-) stratum. Indeed, in this
case, efficacy appears wrongly in this stratum because of the presence of patients who are
actually  marker  (+)  patients,  in  whom  the  treatment  is  effective.  Therefore,  given  a  trial
initially created as an interaction trial but finally concluding treatment efficacy in all patients

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
31

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

together, the rate of false negatives generated by the test should be checked and overall
efficacy should not be accepted if this rate is high (above 30%).

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
32

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

3.  Main clinical study designs: critical analysis

3.1  The "marker by treatment interaction" study: the only design
to demonstrate the clinical utility of a test identifying a
presumed predictive marker, and the targeted nature of a
therapy

► Characteristics of the approach

A  single  study  design,  called  "marker  by  treatment  interaction  design",  allows  determining
concomitantly  the  effect  of  the  treatment  in  marker  (+)  and  marker  (-)  patients,  and  as  a
corollary,  permits  the  existence  of  a  “marker  by  treatment”  interaction  to  be  tested.  This
design  is  the  only  one  that  can  simultaneously  demonstrate  the  three  conditions
validating  the  clinical  utility  of  a  diagnostic  test  identifying  a  presumed  predictive  marker
(3,16–19). As a corollary, it is also the only one that can demonstrate that a treatment may
be actually qualified as targeted, and not merely stratified.

Inasmuch  as  only  this  study  design  can  demonstrate  the  clinical  utility  of  a  test,  it  leads to
results with the highest level of evidence and represents the gold standard in its field.

This approach can be envisioned in the majority of situations:

  before  any  demonstration  of  a  benefit  of  the  treatment.  A  joint  development  of  the
treatment and the test must be logically envisaged. The focus is on the treatment and the
purpose of the dossier is to claim the benefit of the treatment in a subpopulation selected
for the marker;

  after  a  post-hoc  search  in  subgroups  for  a  predictive  marker  following  an  inconclusive
trial  on  the  benefit  of  a  new  treatment.  These  exploratory  post-hoc  results  must  be
prospectively confirmed. For that purpose, an interaction design is appropriate.

However,  confirming  the  presumed  predictive  value  of  a  candidate  marker  identified  in  a
post-hoc search in subgroups following a study with conclusive results cannot be envisaged,
for  ethical  reasons.  In  fact,  it  is  not  reasonable  to  envisage  randomising  patients  again
versus  the  comparator  of  the  first  trial  after  the  new  treatment  has  demonstrated  greater
efficacy in all patients independently of their status for the marker.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
33

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

Figure 6 – Illustration of the "marker by treatment interaction" study design.

► Study design

This design consists of a double randomisation, one in each of the subpopulations iden-
tified by its positive or negative status for the marker. This approach allows comparing the
new treatment N with a comparator C for both marker (+) and marker (-) patients since two
estimates of the effect of N versus C are produced in marker (+) and (-) patients (see

Figure  6).  Comparing  them  allows  searching  for  a  statistically-significant  difference,
indicating, if applicable, the existence of a marker by treatment interaction.

Different  analysis  strategies  may  be  implemented.  In  general,  a  sequential  approach  is
used,  beginning  with  the  interaction  test.  If  this  test  is  not  significant  according  to  a
predefined  threshold,  the  marker  (+)  and  marker  (-)  groups  are  combined  to  do  an  overall
assessment of treatment efficacy. If the test is significant, both strata defined by the marker
are  analysed.  Freidlin  and  Simon  (20)  also  propose  dividing  the  alpha  risk  between  the
overall test and the test by stratum.

► Discussion regarding this approach

Note that in this design, only patients with a perfectly assessable marker are included. The
percentage of patients with non-exploitable results of test must be taken into account in the
critical reading of the results.

It is also important to note that, if the treatment is really effective in a large proportion of the
patient  population,  this  design  will  allow  drawing  conclusions  even  if  the  marker  is  not
predictive.

The ethical issues raised by the investigation of a potential lack of efficacy of the treatment
in  marker  (-)  patients  are  similar  to  those  raised  by  the  randomisation  in  clinical  trials  in
general.  The  justification  of  this  randomisation  from  an  ethical  point  of  view  relies  on  the
principle of equipoise: conducting a randomised clinical study is justified when the medical
community  cannot  determine,  according  to  the  fundamentals  of  evidence-based  medicine,
the best option between two treatments. Randomisation is only acceptable for as long as this
uncertainty  remains  (21–23).  Marker  (-)  patients  should  not  be  exposed  to  adverse  effects
without any hope of being able to benefit from the treatment they receive. Therefore, it must

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
34

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

be  kept  in  mind,  in  the  context  of  stratified  therapies,  that  the  lack  of  efficacy  of  the  new
treatment in marker (-) patients is not a result known a priori. This is a necessary condition
for  demonstrating  the  targeted  nature  of  the  therapy.  The  hypothesis  tested  is  the  non-
superiority  of  the  new  treatment  to  the  comparator,  placebo  or  current  gold  standard
treatment and, like any hypothesis tested by a clinical study, it will ultimately be rejected or
not. This is why it is recommended to provide, in the statistical analysis plan, for the analysis
of  the  treatment  efficacy  in  all  patients  (Mk+  and  Mk-)  in  addition  to  the  subpopulation
analysis. This position is reinforced by the fact that  there are currently counter-examples
among  the  treatments  that  were  a  priori  claimed  to  be  "targeted  by  construction"  and  for
which the lack of efficacy in marker (-) patients has been challenged. The lack of efficacy of a
treatment in marker (-) patients, which would be predicted by a marker identified by post-hoc
analyses,  is  also  very  unreasonable,  given  the  exploratory  nature  of  the  research  and  the
purely theoretical nature of the mechanism of involvement of the marker in the efficacy of
the  treatment  considered  (see  section  1.4).  In  the  end,  it  appears  that  not  conducting  this
research would generally pose a risk to marker (-) patients by promoting wrongly accepting
markers  that  are not  predictive  or  not  sufficiently  predictive  which  might  then cause a
loss of chance and deprive them of a treatment that would be effective for them.

Finally, for example, this design is the one used in the trial "Marker Validation of Erlotinib in
Lung  Cancer  (MARVEL)"  (NCT00738881),  where  patients  are  stratified  depending  on
whether  or  not  FISH  (fluorescence  in  situ  hybridisation)  detects  tumour  expression  of  the
EGFR.  Then  they  are  randomised  to  either  erlotinib  or  pemetrexed  as  a  second-line
treatment for non-small cell lung cancer.

3.2  The other main study designs: determination of treatment

effect and exploratory identification of presumed predictive
markers

In  practice,  the  types  of  methodologies  used  in  the  majority  of  studies  were  and  are  still
focused on demonstrating the efficacy of new treatments but are not suited to demonstrating
the clinical utility of the associated diagnostic tests.

3.2.1  The "targeted" study design

► Characteristics of the approach

The  "targeted"  study  design  formed  the  basis  of  the  first  registrations  of  treatments  having
prescription  limited  to  a  subpopulation  identified  ("targeted")  by  the  presence  of  a  marker
presumed to predict treatment efficacy. It is still commonly used.

This design is completely appropriate for demonstrating the efficacy of a novel treatment in a
subpopulation of patients defined by the presence of a marker detected by a diagnostic test.
However,  as  it  does  not  demonstrate  explicitly  the  lack  of  effect  in  marker  (-)  patients,  it
does  not  validate  the  clinical  utility  of  the  test  in  question,  nor  allow  claiming  the  targeted
nature  of  the  treatment(24–26).  Indeed,  for  the  reasons  explained  in  section  1.5,  it  is  not
reasonable to consider the lack of efficacy in marker (-) patients as implicit on the basis of
preclinical data and/or a theoretical biological rationale.

When  the  efficacy  of  the  new  treatment  has  been  demonstrated  with  this  design  in  the
subgroup  selected  by  the  test,  and  therefore  in  marker  (+)  patients,  the  marketing
authorisation  in  the  indication  concerned  is  logically  restricted  to  this  subgroup  of
patients  in  whom  treatment  efficacy  has  been  assessed.  In  order  to  comply  with  the
marketing authorisation, the diagnostic test becomes required for the treatment prescription
in order to differentiate between marker (+) and marker (-) patients. Indeed, prescribing the

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
35

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

therapy  to  a  marker  (-)  patient  would  represent  an  off-label  prescription.  It  must  be
emphasised  on  this  point  that  the  prescription  restriction  of  the  marketing  authorisation
should  not  be  considered  a  proof  of  ineffectiveness  of  the  treatment  in marker  (-)  patients,
but  only  the  result  of  the  lack  of  assessment  of  this  efficacy.  In  the  absence  of  clinical
evidence, the principal risk of this design is consequently to cause a loss of opportunity
for marker (-) patients by depriving them of a treatment that could benefit them, in addition
to performing a pointless test.

Ultimately, this design is not suitable for demonstrating the clinical utility of a diagnostic
test associated with a stratified therapy. Nevertheless, case by case, the level of conviction
accorded to the preclinical and early clinical elements during the assessment may support to
some  extent  this  demonstration. This  design  would  basically  be  useful  when  the  preclinical
and/or early clinical data lead to concerns of a particular toxicity of the treatment in marker (-)
patients. However, the clinical utility of the test will not be demonstrated. It may therefore be
preferable,  when  possible,  to  choose  a  "marker  by  treatment  interaction"  design  combined
with a protocol ensuring patients the degree of safety necessary for any therapeutic trial.

► Study design

The  targeted  design,  also  called  “enrichment  design”  consists  of  assessing  the  new  treat-
ment N versus the comparator C only in marker (+) subjects (see

Figure 7).

Figure 7 – Targeted study design.

► Study examples

Example: GRAVITAS study

The  objective  of  the  GRAVITAS  (12)  study  was  to  assess  the  benefit  of  a  clopidogrel
treatment  at  high  dose  compared  with a  clopidogrel  treatment  at  standard dose  in subjects
presenting residual platelet hyperactivity after initiation of clopidogrel standard dose following

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
36

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

implant  of  a  drug-eluting  stent.  Platelet  hyperactivity  was  assessed  using  a  VerifyNow®
device.  This  test  was  conducted  in  5429  patients  and  detected  2214  patients  with  platelet
hyperactivity  on  treatment.  These  patients  were  randomised  between  a  high  dose  and  a
standard dose of clopidogrel for 6 months. No statistically significant difference (p=0.97) was
observed between the two groups regarding the occurrence of cardiovascular event (primary
efficacy endpoint).

The study design is clearly a targeted design. Indeed, only marker (+) patients were included.
The  study  was  inconclusive.  However,  if  it  had  provided  conclusive  results,  it  would  have
been impossible to deduce the clinical benefit of the VerifyNow® test since this study did not
document  the  interaction  or  the  effect  of  a  high  dose  of  clopidogrel  in  patients  without
residual hyperactivity. In fine, the possibility that a high dose of clopidogrel could be equally
effective in subjects with no residual platelet hyperactivity could not have been ruled out.

Example: history of cetuximab (anti-EGFR monoclonal antibody)

The  epidermal  growth  factor  receptor  (EGFR)  is  a  transmembrane  glycoprotein  of  the
tyrosine  kinase  receptor  family.  It  has  oncogenic  properties  and  plays  a  known  role  in  the
onset  and  development  of  cancers.  Cetuximab,  the  first  anti-EGFR  antibody,  was  initially
"targeted  by  construction"  in  order  to  act  as  an  EGFR  inhibitor.  The  expression  of  this
receptor  measured  by  immunohistochemistry  technique  (IHC)  was  then  considered  a
predictive  marker  for  the  efficacy  of  this  treatment.  Given  this  so-called  “by  construction"
targeting  and  the specificity  taken for granted given  the  monoclonal  nature  of the  antibody,
the  lack  of  treatment  efficacy  in  "EGFR-negative"  patients  (i.e.  in  this  context,  the  patients
obtaining a negative detection of EGFR by IHC) was a priori considered implicit. Therefore,
the first studies of cetuximab were performed with exclusive inclusion of patients expressing
this receptor (27). However, later studies have shown that patients not expressing the EGFR
detected by IHC also respond to this treatment (8). Testing for the expression of this receptor
is no longer required in the marketing authorisation for the more recent anti-EGFR antibodies
like panitumumab. This example clearly illustrates our only relative knowledge regarding drug
action  mechanisms  (antibody-dependent  cell-mediated  cytotoxicity  phenomenon)  and  the
performance  of  our  diagnostic
the
explanation, the initial lack of validation of the cetuximab effect in EGFR-negative patients by
an  interaction  design  could  have  led  to  wrongly  not  treating  patients,  resulting  in  a  loss  of
opportunity.

tools  (false  negative  problem?).  Regardless  of

3.2.2  The "marker-based strategy” study design

► Principle and study design

Marker (+)

on the marker

Figure 8 – "Marker-based strategy" study design.

The  "marker-based  strategy"  study  design  consists  of  a  strategy  comparison.  In  fact,  the
strategy of administering a new treatment on the basis of the patient marker status identified
by the diagnostic test is compared with a "standard" strategy, where the standard treatment
is  administered  without  determination  of  the  marker  (3)(see  Figure  8).  The  theoretical
objective  is  to  show  that  the  new  strategy  using  the  assessment  of  the  marker  permits
obtaining  better  results  than  the  standard  strategy.  Therefore,  the  results  obtained  with  the
marker-based  strategy  are  compared  with  those  of  the  standard  strategy  (by  grouping  the
patients treated with the new and the standard treatments). As with the targeted design, this
design does not measure the effect of the new treatment in marker (-) patients. Therefore it
does not demonstrate the clinical utility of the diagnostic test or, as a corollary, the targeted
nature of the new treatment.

► Discussion regarding the approach

In reality, despite its name, this design does not allow demonstrating in the strict sense the
superiority of a stratified marker-based treatment strategy - where the choice of the treatment
would  depend  on  the  result  of  the  diagnostic  test  -  in  comparison  with  a  standard  non-
stratified strategy. Indeed, a better result with the marker-based treatment strategy assessed
relative  to  the  standard  non-stratified  strategy  can  be  observed  with  this  design  if  the  new
treatment  is  more  effective  than  the  standard treatment  in  marker  (+) patients,  but  also if
the new treatment is more effective than the standard treatment in all patients, regardless of
whether they are marker (+) or (-); in other words whether or not the test is clinically useful.
Consequently,  a  study  using  this  design  and  having  conclusive  results  only  permits
drawing  conclusions  about  the  superiority  of  the  overall  strategy  based  on  the  use of
the  marker  and  the  new  treatment,  relative  to  the  use  of  the  standard  treatment  in  all
patients. Yet, it does not tell us whether or not, in the context of the marker-based strategy,
patients should be selected to receive the new treatment depending on the presence of the
marker.  Ultimately,  we  should  be  particularly  careful  of  this  design  when  the  primary

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
38

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

objective  is  to  validate  the  marker  and  not  the  efficacy  of  the  new  treatment  (like  in  the
example  of  the  ARTIC  trial,  see  below).  A  study  using  this  design  and  having  conclusive
results  only  permits  drawing  conclusions  about the  superiority  of  the  strategy  based  on  the
marker and the new treatment relative to the use of the standard treatment in all patients. It
does not show whether or not, in the context of the marker-based strategy, patients should
be selected to receive the new treatment depending on the presence of the marker.

Moreover, since this design does not ensure that the treatment has no benefit in marker (-)
patients,  its  main  associated  risk  is  to  wrongly  exclude  these  patients  from  the  treatment
benefits. This exclusion may cause a loss of opportunity for them in addition to administering
a  pointless  test.  Nevertheless,  in  populational  terms,  the  strategy  based  on  the  marker
remains the most efficient. This design could be modified by also randomising patients of the
control  group  (strategy  without  assessment  of  the  marker)  between  the  new  treatment  and
the standard treatment in order to verify that the new treatment is not more effective than the
standard treatment in all patients (28).

Another  source  of  risk  of  wrongly  denying  treatments  using  this  design  concerns  false
negatives,  when  the  test  for  determining  the  marker  lacks  sensitivity.  In  some  cases,  an
interaction  design  allows  detecting  the  consequences  of  this  type  of  test  defect  by  the
existence of a benefit in marker (-) patients (benefit induced by the proportion of Mk+ patients
included in this stratum due to false negative results. This cannot be detected with a marker-
based strategy design. This is therefore necessary to be particularly vigilant with this type of
design regarding test sensitivity.

Ultimately, in this design, a fraction of patients receive the same treatment in both arms. This
dilutes the treatment effect between the two arms because only the marker (+) patients will
be  able,  if  the  treatment  assessed  is  effective,  to  reduce  the  number  of  events  of  the
strategy. This design is therefore less powerful, in absolute terms, than the interaction or
targeted designs. In contrast, this type of design directly assesses the approach that will be
used in practice if the marker/treatment combination is recommended and therefore perfectly
documents the clinical utility of the strategy based on the marker (but never the clinical utility
of the marker).

► Numerical illustrations

Table 15 numerically illustrates this type of design in a situation where the marker is actually
predictive.  Hypothetically,  the  standard  treatment  effect  is  the  same  for  all  patients  and
characterised by a risk ratio of 0.90. The new treatment has no effect in marker (-) patients
(risk ratio of 1.00) and leads to a risk ratio of 0.60 in marker (+) patients. The proportion of
marker  (+)  subjects  is  40  %.  At  the  end  of  the  trial,  the  comparison  of  the  individualisation
strategy based on the marker versus the standard strategy (use of the standard treatment in
all patients) leads to a risk ratio of 7.8 % / 9.0 % = 0.87, while the use of the new treatment in
all  patients,  including  marker  (-)  patients  in  whom  it  has  no  effect,  would  lead  to  a  poorer
result  (frequency  of  events  of  8.4%  versus  7.8%).  The  strategy  of  individualisation  would
therefore  lead  to  a  better  result  compared  with  the  use  of  the  standard  treatment  (7.8%
versus 9.0%), despite the lack of effect of the new treatment in marker (-) patients.
