► Example from the literature

The  ARTIC  (11)  study  assessed  the  benefit  of  clopidogrel,  an  antiplatelet  treatment,
according to the platelet reactivity of patients who received a stent implant. The marker was
here  directly  the  platelet  reactivity  on  clopidogrel  or  aspirin  treatment  measured  at  bedside
(VerifyNow®  device).  In  case  of  high  residual  platelet  reactivity,  the  antiplatelet  treatment
was  intensified  in  the  adjustment  strategy  group.  Conversely,  no  assessment  of  platelet
reactivity  was  done  in  the  control  strategy  group,  where  patients  were  treated  at  the
discretion  of  the  investigators,  according  to  the  current  recommendations.  The  efficacy
endpoint was cardiovascular events involving revascularisation and stent thromboses at one
year. No benefit of the strategy based on the marker was demonstrated (with a hazard ratio
of 1.13; 95% CI comprised between 0.98 and 1.29; p=0.10).

This  trial  illustrates  the  marker-based  strategy  approach,  although  its  results  were  not
significant.

3.2.3  Subgroup analysis a posteriori

► Respecting the hypothetico-deductive process

Increasingly,  it  appears  quite  clearly  that  the  majority  of  studies  conclude  the  nature  of  a
predictive  marker  from  the  results  of  a  posteriori  marker  searches  (also  called  post-hoc  or
retrospective  searches).  This  approach  is  not  methodologically  satisfactory  since,  as  when
demonstrating  the  efficacy  of  a  treatment  without  involving  markers,  the  validation  of  the
predictive  value  of  a  marker  (or  the  clinical  utility  of  its  diagnostic  test)  must  respect  a
hypothetico-deductive  approach,  i.e.  must  be  done  prospectively  (so  called  ad-hoc  study).
Therefore,  this  demonstration  must  be  obtained  in  one  or  more  “confirmatory  trials”

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
41

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

(ICH terminology) especially implemented to test the joint hypothesis "predictive value of the
marker/efficacy of the treatment".

Without respecting this principle, the following two problems arise:

  without  a  hypothesis,  it  is  not  possible  to  perform  a  double  randomisation  according  to
the  marker  (see  section  on  study  designs)  and  therefore  to  use  a  marker  by  treatment
interaction  design.  The  predictive  value  of  the  marker  and  the  clinical  utility  of  the
associated test thus cannot be demonstrated.

  only the a priori formulation of a hypothesis ensures that there is no statistical multiplicity.
Without this prior hypothesis, there is nothing to ensure that the results do not arise from
an intensive search among many candidate markers, even if these markers were a priori
listed.  This  type  of  approach,  which  is  an  a  posteriori  subgroup  analysis,  is  actually
subject  to  an  uncontrolled  inflation  of  the  alpha  risk  of  false  discovery.  It  is  akin  to  a
purely  exploratory  approach,  which  may  be  useful  or  even  necessary  to  identify
candidate markers of interest, but must be limited to the generation of hypotheses to be
validated by ad-hoc confirmatory studies.

► Subgroup analysis - Limits

Exploratory analyses by subgroups are not suited to searching for predictive markers due to
their  exploratory  (not  hypothetico-deductive)  nature  and  the  inflation  of  the  alpha  risk  that
they generate.

The limits of subgroup analyses are mainly known in cases of looking for arguments in favour
of the efficacy of the new treatment when the trial is not conclusive by itself at the level of the
entire included population. This approach then consists of looking for a statistically significant
result in favour of the efficacy of the new treatment at the subgroup level. The major problem
is  that  it  leads  to  multiple  statistical  comparisons  and  alpha  risk  inflation,  making  the
results uninterpretable. By multiplying the number of subgroups, the alpha risk (risk of a type
I  error)  of  wrongly  concluding  treatment  efficacy  in  a  particular  subgroup  is  uncontrollably
increased.  Accounting  for  this  phenomenon  requires  establishing  beforehand  the  list  of
subgroups  that  will  be  tested,  as  well  as  providing  a method for  controlling  alpha  risk
inflation,  such  as  the  hierarchical  sequential  method,  or  the  Bonferroni  correction.  In
practice, these precautions are rarely taken (29–33).

In the search for predictive markers, it is not the uncontrolled inflation of the alpha risk (type I
error), but rather that of the beta risk (type II error) that poses a problem. Indeed, subgroup
analyses uncontrollably increase the beta risk (type II error) of wrongly not demonstrating the
efficacy of a new treatment. Thus, in the presence of an effective treatment in all patients, the
exploration of several subgroups will always eventually find non-statistically significant results
in a subgroup. If these results are used to conclude the discovery of a predictive marker, the
risk  of  wrongly  recommending  a  marker  that  is  actually  useless  becomes  greater  than  the
normally accepted risk (5%).

This  problem  is  particularly  well  illustrated  by  the  example proposed by  Rothwell  (31). This
illustration  highlights  the  risky  side  of  conclusions  coming  from  subgroups.  Figure  9  shows
the results obtained in subgroups created as a function of the day of birth of patients enrolled
in  the  ECST  trial.  This  trial  demonstrates  the  benefit  of  carotid  endarterectomy  in  patients
with  a  symptomatic  tight  stenosis.  However,  this  benefit  disappears  for  patients  born  on
Sunday,  Tuesday,  Thursday  and  Saturday.  The  complete  absence  of  biological  plausibility
clearly shows that results from subgroups can randomly lead to unreal results and erroneous
conclusions. Note that for subjects born on Sunday, the effect is close to zero, which would
be  the  ideal  result  in  a  search  for  a  predictive  marker.  Furthermore,  this  phenomenon  is
amplified by the mathematical reduction of power that occurs at the level of subgroups which,
by  definition,  always  have  fewer  subjects  than  the  global  trial.  It  therefore  becomes  very
probable that statistical significance will not be achieved. Moreover, this smaller size leads to

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
42

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

reduced  statistical  precision,  which  favours  random  fluctuations,  and  to  obtaining  a  hazard
ratio very close to 1 even in the case of a treatment that is still effective in this subgroup.

Figure  9  -  Example  of  results  from  subgroups  showing  increased  beta  risk  (type  2  error)
associated  with  subgroup  analysis:  analysis  of  the  benefit  of  carotid  endarterectomy  in
patients included in the ECST trial with symptomatic stenosis ≥70% depending on their day of
birth (ARR: absolute risk reduction). (According to (31)).

In  this  first  example,  the  absence  of  interaction  (heterogeneity)  will  prevent  unreasonable
conclusions.  However,  the  interaction  test  is  itself  subject  to  alpha  risk  inflation  if  repeated
(like any statistical test). Therefore, if many subgroup explorations are conducted, statistically
significant interactions will eventually always be wrongly observed.  Figure 10 illustrates this
from the same trial, by showing interactions obtained by crossing the severity of the stenosis
and the month of birth. Here again, the total lack of biological plausibility of the results shows
the unique role of chance in the genesis of these interactions.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
43

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

Figure 10 - Influence of the width of confidence intervals on the statistical significance of the
interaction (at constant point estimates) (According to (31)).

These counter  examples  illustrate  the  danger  of repeated  subgroup analyses for  searching
for predictive markers. To prevent alpha and beta risk inflations, subgroup analyses should
make  use  of  appropriate  statistical  methods  involving  a  priori  planning  of  these  analyses.
Candidate markers as well as the direction of the interaction must have been fully identified
in  the  protocol  of  the  trial.  Publication  prior  to  the  trial  completion  date  is  indispensable  to
justify  the  prospective  nature  of  the  research  (contrary  to  a  post  hoc  exploration  upon
learning the trial results).

► A posteriori search for a predictive marker after a conclusive trial

This first situation is likely to arise when after a trial showing the efficacy of a new treatment
in  a  non-selected  population  of  patients,  a  post-hoc  search  for  the  determinants  of  the
response  leads  to  suspecting  that  the  treatment  would  in  fact  really  be  effective  only  in
certain patients who carry a defined marker.

This  situation  poses  the  methodological  problems  of  post-hoc  approaches,  in  particular
statistical  multiplicity  and  may  result  in  inflation  of  the  beta  risk  of  wrongly  not  showing
evidence  of  treatment  efficacy  in  marker  (-)  patients. These patients  could  then  wrongly  be
excluded  from  the  treatment  while  they  were  initially  included  in  the  trial  that  had

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
44

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

demonstrated  the  treatment  efficacy  without  selecting  patients  according  to  their  status  for
this marker. An ad-hoc trial is therefore needed to confirm these results.

However,  an  ethical  question  may  be  raised  in  this  context.  Indeed,  the  initial  trial  led  to
conclusive  results  while  conducted  according  to  a  standard  non-stratified  study  design.
Consequently,  the  new  treatment  became  the  gold  standard  treatment  in  the  area
concerned. To randomise  again  patients  between the  new  treatment  and a placebo  (or  the
previous  gold  standard  treatment)  poses  thus  an  ethical  problem.  A  marker  by  treatment
interaction design cannot therefore be recommended, especially with regard to marker (+)
patients who cannot reasonably be randomised again versus the initial comparator since the
purpose  of  the  confirmatory  study  would  be  to  show  that  the  treatment  is  only  effective  in
them.  Finally,  the  search  for  a  predictive  marker  following  a  conclusive  trial  in  the  general
population most often yields exploratory results not confirmed prospectively and therefore a
level  of  evidence  unacceptable  for  demonstrating  the  clinical  utility  of  the  diagnostic  test
associated with the marker.

However,  there  can  be  situations  where  a  strong  rationale  may  appear  in  the  sense  of  a
probable  lack  of  treatment  efficacy  in  patients  who  do  not  carry  a  marker,  thus  ethically
justifying conducting a prospective study to confirm (or refute) this presumed lack of efficacy
in  these  patients.  In  this  case,  the  approach  to  follow  is  that  of  a  prospective  non-
superiority study. This study may be designed as a targeted study only including marker (-)
patients,  or  as  a  "marker-based  strategy"  study,  with  a  "standard  strategy"  arm
corresponding to the use of the new treatment without identification of the marker. The trial
must  have  a  high  metrological  quality,  since  the  major  risk  is  to  wrongly  exclude  the
subpopulation of marker (-) patients from the treatment. If the lack of a clinical benefit from
the treatment in marker (-) patients is confirmed, it is possible to conclude the clinical utility of
the  test,  thus  providing,  in  accordance  with  the  definition  of  clinical  utility,  optimisation  of
patient  management,  particularly  by  avoiding  pointlessly  exposing  patients  to  potential
adverse effects without any benefit and allowing a potentially more effective therapy, if there
is one, to be used instead.

► Post-hoc search for a predictive marker after an inconclusive trial

Searching  for  a  predictive  marker  after  obtaining  inconclusive  results  in  a  trial  assessing  a
new treatment poses substantial methodological problems. It particularly carries a high risk
of artefactual discovery of non-predictive markers, which may wrongly lead to depriving
certain  patients  of  the  benefit  of  a  new  treatment  and  complicating  the  management  of  all
patients by conducting a pointless test.

Indeed,  in  the  context  of  an  inconclusive  trial,  searching  for  a  predictive  marker  generally
results  directly  from  the achievement  of  this  unsatisfactory  result,  which  leads to proposing
the hypothesis that the efficacy is not ubiquitous but limited to a certain type of patient. This
search  is  then  undertaken  by  a  retrospective  data  analysis,  which  had  not  been  initially
planned. The same data will also be used as a demonstration of the predictive value of the
marker, creating a tautological situation. Moreover, following inconclusive results, a post-hoc
approach generates multiple comparisons, leading to alpha risk inflation on the hypothesis of
treatment efficacy, thus providing a second chance of wrongly concluding this efficacy.

Consequently,  as  with  all  subgroup  analyses,  the  results  obtained  in  this  situation  must  be
considered exploratory and require validation by  a  prospective confirmation  study. In this
case, the demonstration of the predictive value of a presumed predictive marker requires the
use of a marker by treatment interaction design.

The  isolated  demonstration  of  efficacy  of  the  new  treatment  in  marker  (+)  patients  using
another  study  design  would  not  rule  out  the  possibility  that  the  first  trial  was  wrongly
inconclusive  in  all  patients  because  of  a  lack  of  statistical  power,  and  then  followed  by  a

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
45

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

second  trial  performed  only  in  marker  (+)  patients  and  conclusive  only  because  of  better
power, whether the marker is predictive or not.

► Prognostic value in the treated group (treatment-only analysis)

It  is  not  rare  to  encounter  therapeutic  trials  where  the  search  for  predictive  markers
resembles  an  exploration  of  risk  factors  for  the  event  corresponding  to  the  endpoint  in  the
group receiving the new treatment. This approach of not including patients from the control
group is called treatment-only analysis. It actually seeks a risk factor for an event rather than
evidence  of  a  change  in  the  treatment  effect  (24).  It  therefore  never  provides  evidence  of
predictive markers and carries the risk of false identification of a specific therapeutic effect in
a subpopulation of marker (+) patients.

Examples: genetic variants of cytochrome P450 and effect of clopidogrel

To  search  for  genetic  variants  of  cytochrome  P450likely  to  influence  the  effects  of
clopidogrel, Mega et al. considered the patients of the group treated with clopidogrel  in the
TRITON TIMI 38 trial (34). Originally, the objective of this trial was to compare ticagrelor with
clopidogrel in the management of acute coronary syndromes. The primary efficacy endpoint
was  the  composite  endpoint  "coronary  events".  For  the  search  for  variability  factors,  the
patients of the clopidogrel group were genotyped to identify those carriers of genetic variants
of  cytochrome  P450  known  to  be  able  to  impair  the  transformation  of  clopidogrel  into  its
active metabolite in healthy volunteers.

The  analysis  consisted  of  comparing  the  frequency  of  cardiovascular  events  between  the
patients  identified  as  carriers  and  non-carriers  of  genetic  variants  for  cytochrome  P450
leading  to  an  impairment  in  the  biotransformation  of  clopidogrel  into  its  active  component
(see Figure 11).

The conclusion of the authors was that deficient genetic variant forms of CYP2C19 could be
a predictive marker for clopidogrel response.

Figure 11 - Frequency of occurrence of cardiovascular events in carriers versus non-carriers of
cytochrome  P450  genetic  variants  in  a  group  of  patients  receiving  clopidogrel.  (According  to
(34)).

This is an improper approach to search for predictive markers for several reasons:



limiting  the  analysis  to  treated  patients  does  not  permit  assessing  the  effect  of  the
treatment  due  to  the  existence  of  confounders.  Only  comparison  with  a  control  group
experiencing the same effects of the confounders can determine the treatment effect;

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
46

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix



the factor identified may very well be a real prognostic factor associated with a reduced
frequency of the events considered with or without the treatment. In this case, finding this
relationship does not mean that the factor in question influences the treatment effect6;
  beyond the limits of the previous point, to interpret a prognostic factor for the occurrence
of  the  efficacy  endpoint  event  in  treated  patients  as  a factor  modifying  the  effect  of  the
treatment  makes  the  strong  assumption  that  after  taking  into  account  all  moderating
factors, the treatment completely prevents the occurrence of the events.

Thus, it would be possible, with this approach, to find predictive markers for a treatment with
no effect (which means with no significant difference demonstrated between the treated and
control  groups).  All  the  prognostic  markers  would  appear  as  being  predictive  of  the
occurrence of the events.

For  these  reasons,  this  approach  is  inappropriate  to  search  for  predictive  markers  and
carries a risk of false discoveries as the history of clopidogrel clearly illustrates (see the box
below).

Box - History of clopidogrel

Many studies similar to the one by Mega et al. (see the previous example) have been conducted. A
meta-analysis of these studies (35) concluded that given the association between this marker and the
events,  it  should  be  used  for  individualisation  of  the  treatment:  "In  conclusion,  the  findings  of  this
collaborative  meta-analysis  demonstrate  that  common  genetic  variants  in  the  CYP2C19  gene  are
associated  with  almost  1  in  3  patients  not  receiving  ideal  protection  from  ischemic  events  when
treated  with  standard  doses  of  clopidogrel  for  PCI.  Given  how  widely  clopidogrel  is  used  to  treat
patients  with  cardiovascular  disease,  determination  of  the  optimal  antiplatelet  treatment  doses  or
regimens for individual patients is needed to tailor therapy appropriately."

However,  in  another  meta-analysis  also  published  in  JAMA,  Holmes  et  al.  (36)  approached  this
question from the point of view of the marker by treatment interaction. For the same genetic variants
previously identified in research works of prognostic value (which they call "treatment-only analysis"),
they sought interaction data from randomised placebo-controlled trials ("effect-modification analysis").
No interaction was confirmed, showing the limits of the "treatment-only” analysis.

6 This point is only a variant of the preceding point and leads to a confounding bias.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
47

4.  Methodology for the assessment by HAS of the

clinical utility of a diagnostic test associated with a
stratified therapy - Illustrative examples

4.1  General considerations

This  section  proposes  the  HAS  (French  National  Authority  for  Health)  principles  for
assessing a diagnostic test to identify a marker presumed to be predictive associated with a
stratified
the
methodological  guide  entitled:  "Companion  diagnostic  test  associated  with  a  targeted
therapy: definitions and method".

therapy.  A  pragmatic  summary  of

these  principles

is  presented

in

The  basic  principle  for  assessment  of  this  type  of  test  by  HAS  resides  in  the  fact  that  the
recognition of the terms "companion test" and "targeted therapy" is based on whether or not
the clinical utility of the test has been demonstrated. In this section, the evidence required
to  demonstrate  the  clinical  utility  of  a  diagnostic  test  associated  with  a  stratified
therapy is presented. This demonstration proves concomitantly the predictive value of the
marker  relative  to  treatment  efficacy  and  the  targeting  of  the  therapy,  and  justifies  the
designation of companion test.

The clinical utility of a diagnostic test is sometimes claimed from arguments or data coming
from  studies  that  do  not  permit  demonstrating  it,  but  which  are  more  easily  obtained  than
rigorous  evidence.  Actually,  recognising  or  not  recognising  this  claim  depends  directly  on
the design used for the study supporting this application.

It is moreover important to emphasise that a rigorous methodological framework meeting
current assessment standards for therapies is necessary. These companion tests are indeed
directly involved in the therapeutic decision (see box below).

To  be  able  to  guarantee  the  clinical  utility  of  a  companion  test  and  concomitantly  the
targeting of the therapy is an important  healthcare issue since there are many risks in the
absence of this assessment:





the risk of wrongly recommending a treatment that is actually ineffective (biased result or
type I error);
the risk of wrongly recommending a treatment that has no clinical benefit (lack of clinical
relevance).

These  first  two  risks  are  identical  to  those  that  must  be  ruled  out  in  the  analysis  of  a
conventional therapeutic dossier.

  The risk of wrongly recommending a stratified strategy involving performing an additional

useless diagnostic test in all patients.

  The  risk  of  wrongly  not  using,  in  marker  (-)  patients,  a  treatment  that  could  have  some

clinical benefit for them, resulting in a loss of opportunity for these patients.

Ultimately, not ensuring the clinical utility of a test identifying the subpopulations concerned
by a stratified treatment may lead to recommending a discriminatory strategy for the use of a
new treatment that is not optimal in terms of population benefit.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
49

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

Box - Conditions required for a rigorous demonstration of treatment efficacy

1)

Internal validity
a)  Absence of bias

i)  Trial free of selection bias: unpredictable randomisation
ii)  Trial free of implementation bias
iii)  Trial free of measurement bias
iv)  Trial free of attrition bias

b)  Statistical reality of the demonstration of efficacy: statistically significant result with controlled

alpha risk
i)  Appropriate management of multiple comparisons
ii)  Primary  efficacy  endpoint  and,  if  multiple  endpoints,  method  for  controlling  alpha  risk  a

priori defined
iii)  No subgroups
iv)  No inflation introduced by intermediate analyses

c)  Hypothetico-deductive validity

2)  Clinical relevance

a)  Clinical relevance of the comparator
b)  Clinical relevance of the efficacy endpoint
c)  Acceptable benefit/risk balance

3)  External consistency

a)  Biological plausibility
b)  Cohesion between the results of the available studies

4.3  Theoretical examples of application

4.3.1  Different possible situations and interpretations for the interaction

This  section  illustrates  the  different  situations  to  which  the  interpretation  of  the  statistical
interaction  between  two  estimates  of  the  treatment  effect  in  marker  (+)  and  marker  (-)
patients leads.

► Influence of the width of confidence intervals on the statistical significance of

the interaction

Figure 12 shows the influence of confidence interval widths on the statistical significance of
the interaction at constant point estimates. It can be seen that the narrower the intervals are,
the more statistically significant the interaction is. The reason is that the more precisely  we
know  the  respective  treatment  effects  in  both  strata,  the  easier  it  is  to  discriminate  them,
since  the  confidence  intervals  have  increasingly  fewer  values  in  common.  In  fact,  there  is
statistically significant interaction as soon as the confidence interval of one does not include
the point estimate of the other.

Stratum

Marker +
Marker -

Stratum

Marker +

Marker -

Interaction p=0.44

Interaction p=0.0172

Figure 12 - Influence of the width of confidence intervals on the statistical significance of the
interaction (at constant point estimates).

► Ideal case in favour of the predictive value of the marker

The  situation  shown  in  Figure  13  is  the  ideal  situation.  There  is  a  substantial  and  highly
significant interaction (given the separation of the two point estimates of the risk ratios). The
treatment is effective in marker (+) patients. In marker (-) patients, the point estimate of the
risk  ratio  is  1  and  the  lower  limit  of  the  confidence  interval  is  fairly  close  to  1  (0.96)  which
permits,  given  the  clinical  situation  of  this  treatment  (not  described),  concluding  non-
superiority.  This  situation  is  therefore  the  statistically  ideal  situation  to  favourably  conclude
the demonstration of the predictive value of the marker.

Stratum

Marker +

Marker -

Figure 13 - Ideal case in favour of the predictive value of a marker.

Interaction p<0.01

Note that, overall, in all the patients of the trial ("total"), a significant effect is observed, since
the  high  efficacy  obtained  in  marker  (+)  patients  was  not  completely  diluted  by  the  lack  of
effect in the other patients. This leads to questioning ourselves about the decisional value of
this "total".

► Qualitative interaction

Figure  14  illustrates  a  situation  of  qualitative  interaction,  given  that  the  treatment  is
significantly beneficial in one stratum and significantly detrimental in the other. In all, there is
no effect because the effects in the two strata cancel each other out on average.

Stratum

Marker +

Marker -

Figure 14 - Qualitative interaction.

Interaction p=0.0063

► Existence of an interaction without the marker being predictive

In the situation illustrated by Figure 15 there is a significant interaction, but the treatment is
effective in both strata. It is simply less effective in the marker (-) patients than in the marker
(+)  patients.  This  situation  does  not  lead  to  the  conclusion  that  the  marker  in  question  is
predictive.

Stratum

Marker +

Marker -

Interaction p=0.0385

Figure 15 - Existence of a statistical interaction with a treatment effective in both the marker (+)
and (-) strata.

► Situations where it is difficult to conclude the predictive value of the marker

Figure 16 illustrates the situation that is most difficult to interpret. The treatment is proven to
be  effective  in  marker  (+)  patients.  In  marker  (-)  patients,  the  risk  ratio  is  1,  suggesting  no
effect. However, given the relatively moderate precision of this estimate, it will be difficult to
conclude  non-superiority  and  to  consider  that  a  lack  of  treatment  effect  has  been
demonstrated in marker (-) patients. In fact, the lower limit of the confidence interval is 0.86.
Can it be considered clinically negligible as such and lead to a conclusion of non-superiority?
If  the  real  effect  of  treatment  in  marker  (-)  patients  is  0.86,  it  could  be  a  non-negligible
benefit.  To  deprive  these  patients  of  this  treatment  may  lead  to  a  loss of  chance  for  them.
Moreover, in all patients (marker (+) and (-) patients combined), the treatment is statistically
effective. Is it then possible, given a demonstration of the efficacy of the treatment in all types
of patients combined, to exclude marker (-) patients from treatment?

Stratum

Marker +
Marker -

Figure 16 - Situation where it is difficult to conclude the predictive value of the marker.

Interaction p=0.0423

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
55

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

The situation illustrated in Figure 17 poses the same type of difficulty. There is a significant
interaction and the lower limit of the confidence interval for marker (-) patients is compatible
with a clinically useful benefit. It is therefore not possible to conclude either that the treatment
is only effective in marker (+) patients, or that the predictive value has been demonstrated,
due to the impossibility of concluding non-superiority in marker (-) patients. Moreover, it is no
longer possible to conclude that the treatment is effective in all patients. This trial is therefore
not conclusive since it only positively achieves half of its objective (which was to show that
the treatment is effective in marker (+) patients and not effective in marker (-) patients).

Stratum

Marker +

Marker -

Figure 17 - Another situation where it is difficult to conclude the predictive value of the marker.

Interaction p=0.0433

4.3.2  Various theoretical illustrations in support of dossier assessment

These  examples were  composed  with the  objective  of  clarifying the  principles disclosed.  In
some cases, they refer to real situations when it was possible to identify them.

► Case 1 - Interaction design showing a qualitative interaction

An  interaction  trial  shows  treatment  efficacy  in  the  stratum  of  marker  (+)  patients  with  a
hazard  ratio  of  0.67  [0.47;  0.87].  In  the  stratum  of  marker  (-)  patients,  the  results  show  a
statistically  significant  detrimental  effect  with  HR=1.32  [1.01;  1.61].  The  interaction  is  very
significant with p<0.01.

This  qualitative  interaction  showing  a  detrimental  effect  in  Mk-  patients  demonstrates  the
clinical utility of the marker, the benefit of the treatment being also demonstrated in Mk+
patients.

However, the potential of the treatment to have a detrimental effect, as shown by the result
obtained  in  the  Mk-  stratum  should  generate  a  real  suspicion  regarding  the  treatment.
Therefore, the mechanisms leading to this detrimental effect should be well explored in order
to be sure that the treatment has a favourable risk/benefit balance in marker (+) patients.

► Case 2 - Satisfactory interaction design

A marker by treatment interaction trial led to the following results:

Stratum

Hazard ratio (95% CI)

Marker +

Marker -

0.68 [0.38; 0.88]

1.01 [0.91; 1.11]

Interaction p=0.02

The  clinical  utility  of  this  marker  is  directly  demonstrated  by  these  results  due  to  the
demonstration of a statistically significant interaction, associated with a demonstration of the

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
56

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

benefit  of  the  treatment  (considering  the  trial  presenting  no  methodological  limitation
otherwise). The result in marker (-) patients permits concluding non-superiority if in the field
of the trial a reduction by less than 10% of the efficacy endpoint can be considered to be a
clinically insufficient benefit.

► Case 3 - Interaction design with unacceptable demonstration of non-superiority

A marker by treatment interaction trial led to the following results:

Stratum

Hazard ratio (95% CI)

Marker +

Marker -

0.68 [0.38; 0.88]

0.98 [0.72;1.38]

Interaction p=0.036

It is also known that the comparator in this trial reduces the same efficacy endpoint by 15%
(HR=0.85, 95% CI between 0.70 and 0.98).

These results show the interaction and benefit of the treatment in marker (+) patients but do
not  conclude  the  lack  of  effect  in  marker  (-)  patients.  Indeed,  the  optimistic  limit  of  the
confidence interval is 0.72, corresponding to a potential reduction of this efficacy endpoint of
28%, which cannot be considered clinically negligible compared with the benefit provided
by the comparator treatment or the treatment in the other stratum.

► Case 4 - Post-hoc identification by subgroup analysis

The  pivotal  study  for  a  new  treatment  in  oncology  does  not  show,  compared  with  the
standard  treatment,  a  statistically  significant  difference  in  progression-free  survival  that
was  its  primary  efficacy  endpoint  (HR=1.05,  p=0.68).  In  contrast,  an  ancillary  genetic
analysis, performed on 67% of the subjects included in the trial, shows that, for carriers of a
given  genetic  variant,  the  treatment  statistically  significantly  reduced  the  total  mortality
compared  with  the  standard  treatment  (HR=0.40,  p=0.014)  with  a  statistically  significant
interaction  between  this  marker  and  the  treatment  effect  (p=0.023).  No  interaction  was
detected for any other genotypes studied in any of the study efficacy endpoints.

This  result  was  used  to  support  the  claim  of  a  major  benefit  of  this  treatment  (reducing
mortality) in marker (+) subjects.

This claim cannot be accepted since it is based on a result of subgroup analysis performed a
posteriori  and  likely  undertaken  for  purposes  of  demonstrating  the  benefit  of  the  treatment
after  a  primary  negative  analysis.  The  approach  is  not  hypothetico-deductive  since  the
hypothesis of the interaction (and its direction) was not made a priori, and the confirmation of
this hypothesis (the treatment is effective in marker (+) patients) is based on the same data
as  those  that  were  used  to  generate  the  hypothesis.  Moreover,  this  result  also  poses  a
problem of multiple statistical comparisons with a multiplication of statistical tests according
to the different genotypes and endpoints tested. The fact that this result is obtained on total
mortality does not increase its level of evidence (total mortality is subject to random sampling
fluctuations  like  all  other  endpoints),  nor  does  the  fact  that  the  effect  is  greater  (to  be
significant in a subgroup, where the power is necessarily less than that of the trial because
there are fewer subjects, an effect must be greater than the expected effect).

► Case 5 - Subgroup results confirmed prospectively by a targeted trial

A first trial for a new treatment  versus placebo in a non-targeted population of 756 patients
was not conclusive for its primary efficacy endpoint, total mortality, with an HR=0.87, p=0.15,
95% CI between 0.71 and 1.09. An ancillary genetic analysis, performed on 70% of patients,
suggests  that  the  treatment  could  be  effective  for  a  genetic  variant  X  with  an  HR=0.47,
p=0.038.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
57

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

To confirm this hypothesis, a new methodologically satisfactory trial  was undertaken only in
patients  carrying  genetic  variant  X  with  the  same  primary  efficacy  endpoint.  Nine  hundred
and  eighty-five  patients  were  included  in  this  new  trial,  which  proved  conclusive  with
HR=0.88, p=0.012. The result of this trial was used to claim the benefit of this treatment and
the predictive value of the genetic variant (marker) X.

Treatment efficacy is well demonstrated in the subpopulation of patients carrying the genetic
variant  X  (trial  quality,  validation  of  confirmation  trial).  However,  this  approach  does  not
permit  showing  the  clinical  utility  of  the  diagnostic  test  identifying  the  genetic  variant  X.
Actually,  it  cannot  be  ruled  out  that  the  second  trial  was  conclusive  due  to  a  better  power
(more  patients  and  higher  baseline  risk)  and  not  because  the  treatment  is  really  more
effective in these subjects. The elements in favour of this hypothesis are the same size effect
obtained  in  both  trials  (HR  of  0.87  and  0.88)  versus  the  extreme  HR  value  obtained  in  the
subgroup from the first trial (HR=0.47).

This  situation  carries  a  higher  risk  of  leading  to  a  pointless  test  being  conducted,  wrongly
recommending the marker, and depriving patients of a treatment that could be beneficial to
them. An interaction design test is strongly preferred. The inclusion of patients in the marker
(-) stratum should not pose a problem since the trial is undertaken to show that the treatment
is effective in them.

► Case 6 - Identification of the marker by a retrospective analysis of a conclusive

trial

The  benefit  of  a  treatment  was  clearly  demonstrated  by  an  academic  trial  (HR=0.81,
p=0.034)  and  it  was  registered  using  this  trial.  When  it  became  generally  used  in  clinical
practice,  a  post-hoc  subgroup  analysis  of  this  trial  was  undertaken  to  look  for  genetic
markers likely to predict treatment response. This analysis  was performed from the genetic
material collected at the start of the study in all patients. Although planned at the beginning of
the study, the hypotheses for this analysis had not been clearly defined. The objective of this
ancillary  study  was  overall  to  search  for  genetic  determinants  of  response.  From  the
analysis, a single marker from among 14 tested arrived at a statistically significant interaction
(p=0.02)  with  marker  (+)  patients  (HR=0.56,  p=0.02)  and  marker  (-)  patients  (HR=0.98,
p=0.87).  The  conclusion  given  to  this  analysis  was  to  restrict  the  use  of  this  expensive
treatment to marker (+) patients.

However,  this  conclusion  raises  several  problems.  Firstly,  it  is  a  retrospective  analysis,  not
hypothetico-deductive.  Secondly,  it  poses  a  problem  of  multiplicity  with  a  “multiple  testing”
approach among 14 possibilities of wrongly finding an interaction with a risk of 5%. This may
have  harmful  consequences  since  the  trial  initially  demonstrated  a  benefit  in  the  entire
population. Indeed, any restriction of the target population would therefore be associated with
a risk of depriving treatment for patients of the population assessed during the first trial. To
be able to guarantee that this restriction would not lead to a notable loss of chance for these
subjects,  it  should  be  demonstrated  that  the  treatment  is  ineffective  (or  nearly  so)  in  these
marker (-) patients. This requires analysing the confidence interval for the marker (-) patients.
In these subjects, this treatment effect is HR=0.98; 95% CI [0.61; 1.42] (while in marker (+)
patients  it  is  HR=0.56 [0.16;  0.96]).  In view  of  this  interval,  it  is  not  reasonable to conclude
that the treatment has no effect in marker (-) patients. Indeed, this interval is compatible, at
best, with an HR of 0.61, which represents a substantial benefit. It is therefore not possible to
rule out with a 5% risk of error that the treatment could be very effective in these subjects. It
should be noted that in this situation, a p value very close to 1 very likely does not mean that
the treatment does not have any effect but simply that the confidence interval is broad.

► Case 7 - Targeted design

The  marker  used  for  the  pivotal  trial  of  the  dossier  is  considered  a  key  element  in  the
pathophysiology  of  the  disease  and  in  the  mechanism  of  action  of  the  treatment.  The

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
58

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

compound was synthesized to act on this target (see "targeting by construction" section 1.5).
The target was considered at this time as a priori not present in all patients but only in some
of them.

The trial is designed as a targeted trial including only marker (+) patients and comparing the
new treatment versus placebo "on top" the usual double blind strategy. The primary efficacy
endpoint of the study was total mortality. It shows a relative reduction of 20% of mortality with
the new treatment without any detrimental effect. The study also meets all the requirements
for demonstrating the efficacy of the treatment (protection from biases, control of alpha risk).

In  all,  this  trial  is  completely  acceptable  to  validate  the  demonstration  of  the  benefit  of  this
treatment in marker (+) subjects. However, it does not allow claiming the clinical utility of the
diagnostic  test  identifying  the  marker.  Indeed,  the  clinical  data  provided  do  not  allow  ruling
out that the treatment could actually provide a substantial benefit in all patients.

4.3.3  Examples of cases from the literature

► Example of targeted study design - Trastuzumab and breast cancer

Trastuzumab (Herceptin®)  is  a  monoclonal  antibody  whose  synthesis  was  directed  against
the  HER2  receptor  (human  epidermal  growth  factor  receptor-2).  Given  the  property  of
specificity  attributed to monoclonal  antibodies,  it  was implicitly  considered  during  its  clinical
development that it could only be effective in patients with HER2 positive breast cancer. This
treatment  was  consequently  only  assessed  in  targeted  trials  only  including  women  with
HER2-positive breast  cancer  (5,37). This  type  of  trial  design  was chosen  on  the  basis  of  a
mechanistic  rationale,  since  the  demonstration  of  the  interaction  was  considered  to  be
implicit.

However,  the  results  of  retrospective  analyses  conducted  on  several  prospective  studies
more  recently  suggested  that  trastuzumab  could  also  be  effective  in  the  absence  of
detectable HER2 expression. An explanation could be the concept of hidden HER2 receptors
(6,7).

These  observations  led  to  setting  up  the  "TREAT  CTC"  (TRastuzumab  in  HER2-negative
Early  Breast  Cancer  as  Adjuvant  Treatment  for  Circulating  Tumor  Cells)  trial  currently  in
progress (NCT01548677).

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
60

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

► Example of marker by treatment interaction study design - MARVEL trial

The  MARVEL  trial  (NCT00738881)  is  a true  marker  by  treatment  interaction  design  in lung
cancer, stratified on patient status for amplification of the EGFR gene assessed by FISH.

► Example of marker-based strategy study design - SPANISH trial

The  SPANISH  (38)  trial  is  a  strategy  comparison  trial  comparing  a  strategy  based  on  the
mRNA level of ERCC1 mRNA with a standard strategy.

► Example of post-hoc subgroup analyses following a conclusive trial -

Cetuximab in metastatic colorectal cancer

Cetuximab  was  registered  by  the  FDA  in  July  2012  "for  use  in  combination  with  FOLFIRI
(irinotecan,  5-fluorouracil,  and  folinic  acid)  for  first-line  treatment  of  patients  with  KRAS
mutation-negative  (wild-type),  EGFR-expressing  metastatic  colorectal  cancer  (mCRC)  as
determined by FDA-approved tests."

This indication was obtained following a retrospective analysis of the results of the "Crystal"
trial.  The  Crystal  trial  (NCT00154102)  (39)  consisted  of  an  open-label  comparison  of
cetuximab  with  no  cetuximab  as  an  add-on  to  the  so-called "FOLFIRI"  combination  in
metastatic  colorectal  cancer  in  patients  expressing  the  EGFR  receptor  and  not  having
received  prior  chemotherapy.  Patients  were  included  regardless  of  their  status  for  KRAS
activating mutations  and  no  subgroup  analysis  according  to  mutation status was planned  a
priori. Progression-free survival (PFS) was the primary efficacy endpoint.

In  the  unselected  population  of  patients  included  (Mk+  and  Mk-),  a  statistically  significant
improvement of PFS was observed with cetuximab, with a HR=0.85 (95% CI between 0.74
and 0.99), p=0.036.

With a subgroup analysis as a function of the KRAS mutation status, performed in only 45%
of  patients  included,  a  statistically  significant  interaction  was  observed  for  a  secondary
endpoint, tumour response (p=0.03). No statistically significant interaction was observed for
the primary endpoint, PFS, p=0.07, or total survival, p=0.44.

These  data  ultimately  led  to  restricting  the  indication  of  cetuximab  to  patients  with  non-
mutated KRAS, despite many methodological limitations:

  non hypothetico-deductive approach / retrospective analysis;
  no statistical significance for the interaction on the primary efficacy endpoint;
  very  small  size  of  the  marker  (-)  subgroup  (KRAS  mutation)  representing  6%  of  the

patients included;

  broad  confidence  interval  in  marker  (-)  patients,  encompassing  the  entire  confidence

interval for the primary efficacy endpoint in the overall population.

A publication in the NEJM in 2008 (40) presented a post-hoc analysis of the CO.17 trial (41),
which  tested  the  “KRAS  gene  mutation”  marker  on  cetuximab  efficacy  (versus  palliative
care).  Initially  the  CO.17  trial  had  shown  the  efficacy  of  cetuximab  by  reduction  of  total
mortality  (HR  0.77;  95%  CI,  0.64-0.92;  p=0.005)  in  572  patients  expressing  the  EGFR.  A
post-hoc analysis was done on a sample of 394 patients and showed an HR of 0.55 (95% CI,
0.41-0.74;  p<0.001)  in  patients  with  no  KRAS  mutation,  versus  0.98  (95%  CI,  0.70-1.37;
p=0.89) in those with a mutation of this gene. The interaction was very statistically significant
with p=0.01.

Ultimately,  in  2009,  on  the  basis  of  a  systematic  review  of  five  trials  with  cetuximab  and
panitumumab,  ASCO  (42)  recommended  that  patients  with  a  mutation  (codon  12  or  13)  of
KRAS gene should no longer receive anti-EGFR antibody.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
61

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

4.4  Regulatory considerations

The issue of technological assessments in the field of "targeted" therapies is recent but has
fast  gained  in  importance  due  to  the  increasing  number  of  therapies  that  have  become
available  on  the  market  in  the  past  few  years  and  the  considerable  number  of  compounds
(and  associated  tests)  currently  in  development  in  this  field.  Since  very  recently,  rules  for
assessment are being developed in different countries.

This problem, so far, has mainly been approached from the pharmacogenomic angle. On this
topic, a list of recommendations issued by the FDA for the most part, but also by the EMA,
are available on their websites at:
http://www.ema.europa.eu/docs/en_GB/document_library/Scientific_guideline/2011/07/WC50
0108672.pdf (43) and
http://www.fda.gov/Drugs/ScienceResearch/ResearchAreas/Pharmacogenetics/ucm083374.
htm
Furthermore,  at  the  end  of  2012,  the  FDA  published  a  document  of  recommendations
dedicated to "targeted" clinical studies entitled "Guidance for industry - Enrichment Strategies
for Clinical Trials to Support Approval of Human Drugs and Biological Products", that can be
downloaded from:
http://www.fda.gov/downloads/Drugs/GuidanceComplianceRegulatoryInformation/Guidances
/UCM332181.pdf (44). The approaches described in this guide, however, are not completely
specific  for  the  issue  of  predictive  markers,  but  are  more  generally  methodological
recommendations concerning clinical trials.

HAS / Department of Health Technology Assessment / February 2014
62

Companion diagnostic test associated with a targeted therapy: scientific appendix

Conclusion - Perspectives

The concept of stratified medicine consists of a still recent therapeutic approach relying on
"drug/diagnostic test" associations. Within this approach, the diagnostic test has the theo-
retical objective of selecting only patients in whom the treatment is likely to provide a benefit
and thus limit the use of this treatment to these patients. This selection is based on the pa-
tient status for a presumed predictive marker detected by the diagnostic test.

The  concepts  of  targeted  therapy  and  companion  test  fall  within  the  scope  of  stratified
medicine but are much stricter and therefore more demanding in terms of evidence required.
Thus,  to  claim  that  a  stratified  therapy  may  be  considered  as  targeted  or  that  a  diagnostic
test  may  be  designated  as  a  companion  test,  it  must  be  demonstrated  that  in  addition  to
being effective in patients carrying the marker, the treatment is not effective in patients who
do  not  carry  it.  These  demonstrations  are  also  the  foundations  for  the  validation  of  the
clinical  utility  of  the  test,  that  is  to  say  its  ability  to  improve  patient  clinical  outcome  and
provide  added  value  in  terms  of  optimising  therapeutic  management.  The  use  of  the
treatment and performance of the test are inseparable in this context. The clinical utility of the
test can only be assessed in conjunction with the efficacy of the treatment in the same trial.
Only  the  "marker  by  treatment  interaction"  design  provides  these  demonstrations
simultaneously.  Conversely,  study  designs  determining  the  effect  of  a  new  treatment  in  a
selected  subpopulation  of  marker  (+)  patients,  only  allow  concluding  treatment  efficacy  (or
lack thereof) in this subpopulation. They do not demonstrate the clinical utility of the test.

With  regard  to  HAS,  when  a  diagnostic  test  allows  screening  patients  in  order  to  meet  the
requirements formulated in the marketing authorisation of a treatment, the assessment
may,  depending  on  the  type  of  demonstrations  provided,  lead  to  two  types  of  general
conclusions:



if the clinical utility of the test is not demonstrated:





the test will be considered a "conventional" diagnostic test  to identify the subpopulation of
patients  whose  selection  is  reported  in  the  marketing  authorisation.  Although  without
demonstrated clinical utility, conducting this test will not be less essential from a regulatory
point of view to allow the use of treatment under its marketing authorisation,
the qualification "companion test" cannot be claimed, and the therapy will not be considered
targeted.  In  fact,  since  the  predictive  value  of  the  marker  is  not  proven,  the  possibility  of
efficacy in all patients cannot be ruled out;



if the clinical utility of the test is demonstrated:





the  test  will  be considered  a  diagnostic  test  for identifying  the  patients  whose  selection  is
reported in the marketing authorisation,
the test may be qualified as a "companion test" and the therapy considered to be targeted.

In all cases, the diagnostic performances of the test must ensure the reliability of the results
obtained  from  the  studies  conducted.  Its  implementation  must  provide  quality  controls  and
comply with all current quality standards in the field concerned.

Companion test associated with a targeted therapy: Scientific appendix

Description sheet

Title

Description

Working method

Methodological Guide

On-line publication date

April 2014

Paper publication date

Only available in electronic format at www.has-sante.fr

Objective(s)

To present the theoretical and methodological basis for assessment by HAS of
a  diagnostic  test  to  identify  a  presumed  predictive  marker  associated  with  a
stratified therapy.
To define and present the demonstrations expected by HAS for recognising the
use of the terms "companion” test and "targeted” therapy.
To define and provide methodological tools to demonstrate the clinical utility of a
diagnostic test associated with a stratified therapy.
To illustrate the theoretical aspects with practical examples.
To inform the level of evidence accorded by HAS to the study designs used for
the development of the diagnostic tests concerned regarding the demonstration
of clinical utility.

Requested by

Internal HAS initiative

Sponsor

Project steering

Haute Autorité de Santé (French National Authority for Health, HAS), service
évaluation des actes professionnels (Department of Health Technology
Assessment, SEAP))

Head: Michèle MORIN-SURROCA, Deputy Department Head: Marc
GUERRIER)
